SECRET OF MARK PEPS.

Even a hair throws a shadow, but Mark had not the suspicion of treachery nor a hint of tangible danger. An Indian always prefers subtle attack to apen fighting, and Goya waited his chance with a patience worthy of a better object. When Mark was unencumbered by the ties of matrimony, when he could buckle on his weapons and unswervingly face the enemy like a valiant knight, when he mixed more with the masses and was in daily touch with their aspirations and troubles, his enemies within the gates kept in the background; but so soon as the obligations of an exalted position made demands on his time and kept him almost exclu- sively within the Royal circle, with locked face and black heart Goya began to plot in downright earnest. It is hard to knock over a lie, because it has short legs, broad shoulders and good wind. Go'ya told many lies which, often repeated, were accepted by some as Gospel truth. The result supervened. There were no newspapers in Golden Vale in which those of high estate could read what was said of them, and nip a libel in the bud. For the chatter of irresponsible persons Mark had supreme contempt. Those who called us aliens were busy casting innuendoes, and so much did this exclusive tribe in their hearts dislike pale faces, that none gave us warning. In fact there must have been sly conspirators among the Royal guard, for by the most subtle stratagem plans were laid and carried out so skilfully that we were non- plussed in a trice. We had trodden on a nest of serpents, and the reptiles rose hissing with fury. k One evening I was spending a pleasant hour with my chief and his wife, when a detachment of soldiers surrounded the house, and Pecos ai3pounced that a high military officer had been admitted into the hall and desired an immediate audience. His commands were of a peremptory nature. Olive flew to her husband, and putting her ' aria about his neck, kissed him, and whispered in alarm: "If they have discovered the desecration of the Royal sepulchre we are lost!" Mark hushed her to silence, rose fearlessly, and strode out to demand an explanation and -an apology for this intrusion. Notwithstanding Olive's unpleasant reflections the vanity of the bride asserted itself, for she seized a silver mirror and set her hair in trim before it, then, beckoning me to follow, she tripped out. I stepped into the hall Just as the commander waved his hand and his men formed a cordon about us. Olive threw herself upon her husband's breast, her face betraying signs of dismay. Mark faced the officer and haughtily demanded the nature of his business and the pur- port of the parchment scroll be displayed. "Learn thou, Mark Pepys, consort of the Princess Olive, that Queen Kane hath issued this warrant for thine arrest. The lords military require thy presence three hours after sunrise on the morrow. The indictment sets forth three grave charges, to wit, that thou didst treacherously design to selze some of the Royal treasures; that a secreted plan of the city defences, drawn by thee, bath been found among thy papers; and that thou bast come as a spy and a traitor to seize Queen Kane and keep her for a ransom." Mark looked at the commander fixedly, his face drawn as though be smarted with a sense of great injury. Olive clasped her hands, stared at the document, and then threw her arms around her husband's neck. "By whom am I accused ? " asked Mark, a slight tremour in his voice. The commander lifted his eyebrows with surprise, and shook his head gravely. "The tribunal insists Upon thy safe custody, r, ho politely answered, bowing deferentially. 'An escort neither accuses nor 'defends; their duty is to the tribunal, thine to obey the Royal mandate." He tapped the scroll with his finger tips, which set Olive vageely wondering, as she gazed at the officer with a h elf-awed expression. "Who bath signed the order? " she asked, gripping the parchment with both hands and gazing wildly at the seal and signature. Mark folded his arms, his eyes glistening with defiance. Feces stood in the background yearning to help us in our dilemma, but kept at bay by the soldiers. Olive placed her left hand upon hex throbbing heart and her aiglat upon her burning forehead. "It ie. the Queen's seal and writing!' she exclaimed in a lialf-frightened whisper, looking importunately into the officer's stolid face, and again nestling close to her husband. "Xvil ones ever seek to do good men mischief. Either the Queen bath lost her reason, or this document is a forgery ! " "Princess," replied the commander in a harsh and imperative tone, "I warn thee that all th 3 words and actions . may be repeated in evidence against thy husband. Silence may spare thee much sorrow. Let us go hence." Olive relaxed her hold of Mark, and, facing the officer with much of her mother's dignity, she retorted, "If my husband's accusers were honest, they would not swear falsely against him." The officer bowed and smiled good-temperedly. My heart thumped with excitement, my flesh crept.. I set my teeth and clenched my fingers as I saw s cloud of scorn gather upon my chief s brow. "Mark," I said, in British idiom, "every cool has a richt t' crow upon his own dunghill resistance may land us in the dirt-tub. Folll plaits her am tawse, an' a wuman lays it on thick. Sing snia', an' kick at length." A cold, derisive smile was upon Mark's lips; hit eyes flashed, his whole bearing was one of supreme contempt for my e. I felt angry, and the thought flashed through my mind that the veil which had hidden the secret of his past was being rent. Hie steadfast ge.zi at me telegraphed that lie understood the sombre scrutiny of my eyes. Oh, the torture of that moment! For the hideous aspect of hit threatened degradation and deith was the keenest mental agony. Lucia Diaz was a prophetess! , In a voice, low :lad tremulous, Olive pathlsti- callv begged the soldiers to let her appeal to her mother for mercy, but she was repulsed with looks of disdain. Mark drew her close to himself, and kissed her forehead tenderly. "Have patience, my love," he said softly, "there is always hope while -the heart beats." When brave men shew tenderness, cowards strike. The commander stamped his foot impatiently, and knitted his brow. The soldiers menaced Mark with their weapons, crying : "To the tribunal, or death!" "He mocks our patience!" "Down with the traitor!" as though they were picked men for this purpose. "Oh, pitiless Heaven!" cried Olive, "why for- sake us in our trouble!" The guard closed in with a shew of force. "0 God, wither their arms! Let their eyes be blind and their brains chaided! Stand aside, teamsters! At your peril lay hands on the Princess Royal. Know yo that lam Queen liana's daughter!" "Hush! Olive, their duty is to obey," said Mark, "ours to render their task light.. lam the victim of a plot by disloyal and treacherous men, who strike at the throne through me!" "But there is no truth in tse charges'?" asked Olive excitedly. Every tinge of colour fled froth Mark's face and. lips, for he dare . not give them the lie direct. I sighed audibly, for his life was at stake. Olive's wild loveliness and despair made my heart ache for her. She shielded her husband, caressed him and • tried i ' n vain to persuade the soldiers that -they were deceived. Every minute increased our peril, for others, more threatening, slipped in and barred the doors. We were without 'means of defence, they were armed to the teeth; so all Mark could do was to protest and reluctantly comply. "Sandy," he said, "things look black for ice. I confide my dear wife to your dare. If the worst conies, beg the Queen to give you my pocket-book. . . . Have you a cigar?" he added, with astonishing sang-froid. The officer eyed me suspiciously as I drew out my case and offered it. hfark struck a lucifer and lit the cigar with the utmost coolness, and as the night air was chilly, he pointed to his fur-trimmed cap and overcoat. The officer comprehended, and ordered a man to take everything from the pockets. This act fired Olive with indignation. She stamped her foot, and would have darted forward to stop the search had not a soldier placed the haft of a spear before her and respectfully warned her not to interfere.

When Olive found she was to remain a prisoner in her own house, my admiration for her undaunted spirit was, only eclipsed by the depth of her love for my colleague. With a tierce gesture, her eyes blazing with scorn, she raised her arms and cried, "Let the Heavens rain red-hot dust to blind the eonspintors! Let the earth open and switliow Ott% and ail who aid him!"

At the mention of t.To.)a tile bowlers seized the •incess and dragged her into the room, u.tinE, only such force as was necessary to that end. Mark had walked out with a firm step, and I soon followed close at his heels, for Olive had quietly advised me to ascertain his destination and render what help I could to save him. It was a clear, starlight night, and as the inhabitants were gone to rest we were marched without interruption to the gate of the Royal enclosure. There they stopped me, for I had made up up my mind to share imprisonment with my friend. He shook my hand warmly and said: "Sandy, I trust to your loyalty and courage. Good-night." Left alone outside, I stood to gather my wits, when someone gently tapped my shoulder. Turning round suddenly I saw Pecos making signs for me to silently follow him. I did so, and when we were out of earshot he said the Queen wished to see me. word having been brought to him by a courtier, who entrusted him to hand me the key of Eana's private entrance. This I took, and when Pecos had piloted me to the door, he ran back home to the Princess. As soon as I turned the key the door was opened gently from the inside by the Queen herself! Even 1V the subdued light I could see that uncommon events troubled her. In a low, confidential tone she asked: "How fares the Princess Olive?" "The lady is smitten with grief at her lord's arrest." "The issue of my warrant was his only safe- guard. My harshness appeased his accusers, and gave me the only chance to plan his escape. Come!" We went speedily to her room, where she covered herself with a hooded robe of silk interwoven with feathered devices. She opened a secret door in her room, and we passed to the top of a flight of stone steps. Descending in the dim' light we came to a long subterranean corridor, through which we passed speedily, then ascended a flight of steps and, by a secretly-contrived door, gained access to the citadel where Mark was a prisoner. Evidently %Etna knew her son-in-law's "location," for she made straight for his room—otie set apart for high- class offenders awaiting trial. I noticed' the absence of guards, which distinctly proved that ' certain officers had connived at setting Mark free. The Queen knocked on the door gently with her finger nail. . "Who's there?" Mark asked in the Indian tongue, and without the slightest tremour in his voice. "The Queen," I replied softly in English. "Open quickly: it is I, Sandy." "When the Queen commands," he answered in Indian, "a loyal prisoner should obey"; this ironically. The door was unbolted, and Kana's dumb motions allayed my chief's surprise. We entered quickly, closing the door after us. The room was sparsely furnished, a wax candle burnt upon the bare table, and the rays of a young moon shone upon the tiled floor. "Why does the Queen honour me with this visit," said Mark proudly, "when I suffer degradation at her hands? " He stood at full length, with folded armF, waiting for the answer. Rana eyed him indignantly, her lips compressed, her brow knit. "I risk In y throne for Olive's sake and thine," she replied, precisely in the same tone. . • • _ -

ss„ "I come as a friend—though I am not sure that men lie concerning thee. . . . I had hoped," she added with a touch of bitter sarcasm, "that a man of honour could not stoop so low." Mark's • eyes drooped, and he turned aside, ashamed of himself. -"I signed the warrant to prevent thine assassination, although I knew thou once sought to do me harm." . Mark was staggered by such magnanimity. "A mad resolve, prompted by evil tale-bearers," he said. "Let me know my fate; I am prepared to face it. God help Olive! ' "A plan of the city buildings and defences hath been found among certain papers in thine old quarters. It was no compliment to me that thou didst believe I would receive thy stolen treasure. Had I not enough wealth without coveting thine? But I forgave thee because of Olive's rescue; and for thy youth and bravery I treated thee and thine as honoured guests. But Goya hash worked thy ruin, and embittered the priests against thee." ."Then no living man or woman can save me!" he exclaimed. "This is a sad blow for Olive." A chilling sweat ran down my back as Mark relapsed into a chair, for he now fully recognised the rare and generous instincts of Kane, and the hideous risk he had run. As the Queen approached him, he fell upon a knee, and kissed her hand as a token of submission and repentance. "Mark. the Queen comes to set ye free," said "an' Princess Olive is a party to the scheme." "Trust me to cover thy retreat," said Kana. "I forgave thy foolhardiness, and now my love for Olive and admiration for thy good qualities prompt me to save thee. Evil men rage for thy life. Thou (lost not lack courage, but for Olive's sake thou must escape!" "I cannot forsake my wife!" he gasped. prefer death to separation." "Olive will be safer with me until the excitement wanes," urged Kana. "Your presence is a menace to her. Trust me, and she shall be restored to thee at San Leno, alive and well. Art thou agreeable to follow my advice ?" "Mark, hesitation means death." "I have no alternative," I e answered with a long-drawn sigh. "If it please thee, generous Kane, I yield." , "Then cheat thy foes! My plan for thine escape cannot fail! Follow me; tread lightly, and speak not. Let the candle burn, and close the door gently. I shall need to return, .for a purpose." We three traversed the underground passages, and by a tortuous path through the Royal groves Kana conducted us to the city boundary. At a spot remote from ot servation we passed out by the Queen's Gate, to discover four horses—one a beast of burden—and a trusty Indian guide. At the farewell Kann wept, nl as she disappeared we could hear her sobbing as if her t cart would break, though she stifled her anguish by drawing her hood close about her face. The Indian advised us to fnount speedily, saying that if danger threatened the Princess, she would follow and seek us at a renOesvous agreed upon, among the Sierra Madre. I had my work cut out to screw up Mark's com- pliance with the Queen's request, for when the dread and excitement began to wear off he almost persuaded himself to go back and bring Olive. I knew such a course would mean capture and per- haps death for all of us, so I prevailed upon him to continue our flight. We were provided with weapons and ammunition, besides costly 'knick- knacks for presents. We avoided the straggling cottages, and rode along unfrequented trails, bear- ing south-east to reach the rugged mountains before daybreak. All the night we moved blindly, having faith in Kane. When danger threatened we lay low, and hardly breathed for fear of discovery. Mossy ground underfoot, a crescent moon overhead, the horizon jagged of forest and rock we rode stealthily onward. No man spoke, no light shewed, no chat or smoke, but the grass-muffled tread of our horses, and the moon-veiled figures, forbade the fancy that our escape was all a dream. Torture and death behind us, freedom ahead! Suddenly the warning cry of a suspicious Indian riveted our attention, and as the bold fellow ill- measured, our strength and shewed himself, I slio•ildered my. rifle in deadly earnest and speedily fired. A Maze—a puff—and whizz went the bullet! Phuttl ' With a cry more of dismayed astonish- ment than of pain, the dusky spy leaped into the air and fell on his back with a thud. "A good shot! " exclaimed Mark in a half- whisper. "Let us make a dash for it!" Not a flaw in Kana's plan; not a check tikl. now; not hardly a jolt as we sped from the fanatical priests and cheated the superstitious Indians of their victims. Then the excitement rose red4hot into my head, and I think Mark and the guide caught the infection. Yet Mark thought of Olive, and every step we took was a whip of steel lashing his brain. It seemed cowardly to rush from danger, and vet he knew it was the only course open to save his life and spare his wife suffering. Kane would be equal to the emergency, and save Olive. Mark trusted Kane, for he had once known the depth of his own mother's love. As the first rays of sunrise glinted on the upper ridges we drew bridle, and took our bearings. Thank God, we were safe now; and so we rested awhile. After several days' hard journey we stopped where a waving pine forest was communing with floods of singing water. The merry little ouzel's sweet and tender song flowed from his round breast like a stream over a smooth dome, and then the notes came dashing and strong as if the bird was wild with glee. His music blended with the trills of rapids, the gurgles of a hundred esldies, and the swirl of the sun-bowed spray. Lovable and constant, he neither sought nor shunned our company. "Watch how it flies round and round I the pool to oirk a phy•irl spot. There it aligles! It II swimming about for an easy reach to the

bottom. Down it goes!" cried Mark enthusiasti- cally, as the bird gave a sharp stroke of its wings and disappeared. This was the first time Mark had roused himself from a painful melancholy, for he wondered how Olive fared, and i.,,,,1041 himself for having shirked his responsibility. "Within the bowels of the earth," said he, "the devil stirs his cauldron!"

CHAPTER XXII. • RIGIIT ABOUT FACE. While we travelled, stirring events were tran- spiring among Kana's people, the which it is necessary to briefly set down here, as they were subsequently told me. It appears that after our departure the Queen acted with astonishing skill and diplomacy. By subtle means she obtained possession of the plan drawn by Mark, and burnt the same, stamping the ashes beneath her feet. In this and other cunning ways she reduced Goya's evidence to hard swear- ing, and he„ fearing the consequences of failure, determined not to prefer' the charge of connivance on Princess Olive's part. Our escape WB5 so convincingly accounted for that the large majority of the accusers were amply satisfied when they found the city was rid of as for ever. To confirm, this Rana issued a pro- clamation to the effect that no white men should be permitted within the boundaries of her terri- tory. Olive was primed to say she preferred the love of her kinsfolk to that of any paleface ; but the, e words cost her much secret regret and heartburn- ing. However, her popularity and the Queen's tact prevailed; the guard was immediately with- drawn, and the Princess had liberty to roam as she piensed. But Cloya had a tromp card tip his sleeve, which he now played trlurnp/tantly. Certain grave -hispers among the gossips proved how ctpt may .:eep a secret, hut Itee never. The loss of Kana's royal dead .passed, in ,confldence from house to house, and as the signs of the Great'rpirit'S wrath gathered about the valley Kana grew suspicious, and, not knowing the cause, she summoned a great council. ,There Goya. the man of strength from whom Mark wrested the prize, made a hitter speech, 'saying that woe is the lot of Golden Vale upon the desecration of the bodies in the Rerl eaverns. The knave proposed that Rana, her chief Misistels and priests, should visit the sepulchre at once, and give hearsay the lie direct if the bodies were un- touched. The Lady Olive, hearing whispers of this through Pecos, now made excuses for remaining indoors, and then refused to visit the Royal palace not- withstanding Kana sent messages of welcome, repeated with earnestness, and then as commands. When, therefore, the ugly tongue of scandal *agitated the ruble mind, and a lady of the Court came with greeting, but in reality to learn the reason of Olive's strange conduct, the Princess trembled with fear. "Kana is sad at heart; Pecos averred; "but venom lurks in Kana's hem t."

"The Queen is superstitious," answered Olive; -and if her people wax wild with rage, my nr)ther !area not refuse a victim to appesse their wrath. fherefore, good Pecos, let us gatlur all the money we possess, and such • jewellery as we can con- veniently carry; and, without goorls, extra clothes, or beasts of burden, let us wander forth in broad daylight, to avoid creating susnicion." "Happy thought!" exclaMed Pecos, pleased to see a way out of the dilemma. "With Jip as guide?" "Even so ; for the bloodhound can be trusted, whereas, if we journey forth with servants, Kana's anger will increase. Better go into exile than be disgraced first, and afterwards banished ; for it is an unalterable law in Golden Vale that whosoever accepts • the trust of watching the dead shall be thus punished for lack of vigilance. Cruel though it be, yet even the Royal children share the same fate as the cominon watchers. To lessen tempta- tion, the Royal car erns had no precious stones, gold, or silver deposited therein." "How are the accused watchers disgraced?" "My mother once told me," answered Olive, "that she had seen a young man tied to a tree, flogged, 'and then drummed out of the tribe, while boys and girls jeered and threw dust at him. He I had drunk imlque, and lowered himself below the level of the beasts, on the very day appointed for him to vis:t the step o'ires. Another was cast to. the wild beasts, and hi.A bones burnt in the fire of ilAstruction without th, city." Mive knew it would to useless to throw herself upon the mercy of the Queen if the priests de- manded her punishmenr, and therefore she strolled ?orth with Pecos and dip, neither hastening her steps nor tarrying until nightfall. But Goya hated the Princess because of Mark Pepys's prowess, and he made indecent haste to iirove the truth of his story. So it came to pass that Kana was soon bewailing the loss of her Royal dead and lamenting Olive' apparent lack of diligence. Goya agitated so effectually that all the latent savagery of Indian blood soon shewed itself. "An insult is offered our tribe," argued the demagogue; '.;concocted by Mark Pepys, and winked at by his wife." Therefore a feverish excitement seized the people, fomented greatly by the aspect of their local volcano. If calamity threatened the inhabitants, tradition reasoned that it could only be averted by blood. So racial prejudice was as fusel oil in spirits, stimulating, because fiery, but withal a deadly poison. -So mightily grew the feeling against Olive that it only wanted fanning to work the people into a rebellion. And alas! Olive's error of judgment in not at once shielding herself behind a mother's love—in not pleading for the Queen's forgiveness—tended to alienate sympathy. The best of us make mistakes, and the Princeks was but human. The coming of Mark Pepys, hailed with so much 4* light, was now construed into a visitation of the kl. One. And a half-caste, even though a Prin- 'A, fell in for a larger share of suspicion; for if Indians hated the white races they also feared -4tCm, but they usually despised one of mixed ,iood. In this the women fervently joined, so that the Queen soon found herself and a few honest friends comparatively isolated. For a time her energies seemed paralysed, and not even a con- soling religious belief could eradicate the super- stition begot of ancestral worship. The gossips exaggerated Olive's sin, and a verbal "whip" was issued summoning a council, which the Queen found it expedient not to attend. Measures prompt and drastic were contemplated, and Kane 's motherly feelings got the better of her judgment. The world should pity her; mothers

in particular can unaerstann her replying: "Tell the Ministers and prie.ts that I refuse to sit in judgment on my own daughter. They iinny. depose me, and mete out what punishment they wish, but I will never listen to arguments for the disgrace of my only child." And for evil purposes many cast this answer in, her teeth, that the Queen might be humiliated. Thus, instead of guiding her counsellors, Kane peremptorily defied them, and so gave Olive's enemies their opportunity to sway the multitude, The leaders soon determined upon a policy—to wit, the immediate arrest of the Princess, pending her trial for treason. A band of warriors was sent out with strict instructions to bring Olive, alive or dead. And so it befel that Mark's wife was over- taken in her flight and surrounded. Resistance was useless, pleading also of no avail; threats of Mark's wrath were scoffed at; the anger of Pecos was laughed to scorn. "Mark, Mark!" she cried in great anguish. wringing her hands and wildly gazing eastward in the vain hope of her appeal reaching her husband's ears, "come thou to thy wife's aid, lest this people wreak their vengeance on the innocent. God is witness that the guilt of this desecration lieth neither on thy heart nor on mine." The foot-followers who gathered about the cavalcade yelled with derision, and the military commander, seeing Olive's life in danger, became surly and growled: "Enough of this hypocrisy, Princess! My duty is plain." "Do thy duty," answered Olive, "but without insult. Dian found guilty by the law, I claim the law's protection.' This spirited and dignified answer abashed the commander, though in truth he was severe only to save the Princess. "Thou must return with us, my lady," he spoke in a decisive tone; "we having sworn to guard thee from harm by the way. SO strangle not our patience with much solicitation." "0 Mark, my husband!" murmured Olive in utter despair, "death shall be my lot rather than d isgrace. ' Then suddenly a thought flashed across her mind, and springing towards Pecos she nervously Whispered: "Pecos, we must go back with them; but, if chance offers, send Jip to the east after my husband. No other messenger is safe. Wilt thou promise? " "Aye, lady; trust Pecos." And the retainer bowed deferentially. Then with a sigh of relief the Princess extended her arms towards the commander, saying: "At the Queen's command, I yield." Goya was to the forefront, clearing the way, his black eyes fierce, his rough face exultant. The officer looked with compassion on the Royal captive, but the populace yelled with more impatient ferocity: "Why tarry so long P Let the gods be appeased!" And so Olive was made to retrace her steps, every mile adding to her discomfiture, for her arrest got abroad, and a great multitude came out to witness her return. At their windows women who had bowed the knee before her now shouted epithets in their frenzy; even the children were seized with a wild delirium, and patriarchs of the tribe smote their breasts and cried aloud: "Away. with this sorceress!" "To the judgment hall!" "Let this half-caste be tried for treason!"

Young maidens who had strewn bridal flowers in her path now gnashed their teeth, and would have laid their hands upon her but for the protection of the soldiers. Indeed, those made of sterner stuff now pitied the Princess, and kept the more revenge-. , ful at bay, even striking them and threatening to shoot them if they offered violence "Princess," spoke the commander, "the people are mad; they know not what they are about. Thou art weary and footsore. We will halt, that some provision be made for thy relief." Olive's pale lips moved, but great mental agony arrested her words. Thereupon he set himself to provide her with a horse, for which she tried in vain to thank him, the anxiety of her precarious position having wearied her nigh unto fainting. As for Pecos, he led Jip, and restrained the blood- hound lest he should jeopard'se his mistress by attacking the foremost who threatened to break through the cordon, for some madcaps foamed at the mouth with rage, and spat at the Princess, reviling her unceasingly. She swerved with physi- cal pain, but fought eown the feeling. A dis- hevelled hag threw a rotten orange, which struck the captive's cheek amid the delirious laughter of the ruffians and their women. Some of the soldiers laughed, and the officer's frown was met with the mob's wild gestures and devilish howlings. Olive's.conrageous demeanour won a )me admira- tion, that found no voice for fear of the surging crowd. • Sometimes the Princess would cast her eyes to the skies, and her lips would move as if in prayer. Her dark eyes shone with a divine light, for even in her utmost extremity hope buoyed her' soul. Calm amid that stream of humanity, Olive ' looked down at Pecos and spoke words of good cheer. The deformed Man was so &spited that few took the trouble to throw missiles a', him, and those who pressed around were afraid to kick the faithful hunchback because of Jip's fierce looks; but they irritated the poor beast by throwing stones and tufts of grass at him, from some of which Pecos escaped not, nor from the brutal horse-play t of Goya. And so back to her own home the captive Princess ith was driven---a heroine among wolves who howled 'W for her body that they might tear it to pieces. Her calm courage irritated the swelling mob, who cried mockingly: "Hail, Princess, thou art iongue-tied " "Thy white lard bath forsaken thee!" "A curse on thfel" It transpired, however, that some of the military leaders only meant their show of force to appease the people's rage, so that in calmer moments Olive should secure a fair trial. Thus, when the escort arrived with ,their prisoner at the square in front of the judo-tier t hail, the high priest, who had performed Ilark's marriage ceremony, stood forth upon the stone steps, and, looking down upon such a sea of red faces as the world will never behold apin, he made signs for silence, the which obtained, he cried with a loud voice, the tones reaching even to the very outskirts of the seething mass:

"Ye people of Golden Vale, the Queen-mother sends greeting. Hear ye her messsge. She hath delivered the Princess unto her people to do with as it seemeth best, knowing full well that ye desire- for the Royal prisoner a like justice such as ye would wish for your own offspring. In such dire extremity the Princess cannot defend herself, -and it is the vote of those to whom ye look for counsel that on the morrow the trial shall take place, in this judgment-hall, at the hour of noon or such earlier hour as the heralds shall proclaim. Ye have hitherto won fame for fair dealing, and what time ye would give a. commoner to prepare her defence ye cannot refuse Kana's daughter.. The Queen even now- Roth sick, as though- her heart stoppeth, but the safety of the State comes before even the Royal grief. Nana asks for to mercy that cannot, with justice, be granted. Since, therefore, our Qileen acknowledges the people's right, ye would do well to look with favour on a trial by jury after the next sunrise. What say ye?" The answer was a great howl from Goya and his malignant followers, mingled with many expres- sions of acquiescence; and after much tumult, and when the excited women had screeched themselves hoarse, the more rational among the multitude, who admired Olive's sublime endurance, led off 'the cries for a trial by jury. Thereupon voices were raised : "Agreed! Agreed!" This was followed by a large body marking time with their feet, not, however, as our English soldiers do, but by raising their knees and dexterously slapping the ground with the soles of their feet until a scuffling noise, rhythmical and loud, fell with welcome upon Olive's ears. It was at least a respite, during which some attempt might be made to rescue her.

"God give me courage," she prayed, "in the hour of my trial."

(To be continued.)

LADIES' LETTER

"M Y DEAR NELLIE, I saw the other day a very pretty blouse of pale pink chiffon. It was mounted on pink silk and trimmed in a trellis design with fancy white satin ribbons. A flat band of black velvet was placed at the edge of the square-cut neck and from under- neath the ribbon appeared frills of pink chiffon, the lower one being edged with a tiny riching of the same, and the upper one with white satin ribbon. I need not add that this locks well in many other colours including black.

adopt now e quite a matter of personal taste but I saw one which I thought looked very well. It was a very simple and artistic coil at the back, which was arranged with a tail of hair. The top loop of this design was formed of the wearer's own hair and the three lower ones with the artificial tail. A few short curls might be added, if desired, and I was informed that they could be bought separately.

pardon _ diversion of mine from the s-übject of dress, I should like to tell you of a gauged blouse which a friend of mine was wearing. It was an exceptionally pretty model in maize- coloured glace silk veiled with corded jet black chiffon, into which were let insertions of jet em- broidery. This trimming formed a striped yoke at the back below which there was accordion-pleated black chiffon. The sleeves were corded from _ .

shoulder to waist whilst at the throat and wrists Were tiny ruchings of black chiffon which tended to for to a full jabat in front. The waist band was also of folded glace silk.

I am glad to be able to tell you that transforma- tions are quite a eneciality, and these now take the place of the old-fashioned wigs, which were heavy and cumbersome. This head-dress has only a narrow foundation round the head, and fastens at the back with a hook and eye, and adjusts itself automatically to the size of the head with inser- tions of elastic. I may say that the fringe can be arranged in any style, and long, wavy hair falling over the back of the head, can, if desired, be combed in with the wearer's own hair. As you Will see, this alteration will be invaluable to people With very thin hair or partial baldness. Trans- formations are also worn by many people who have ample tresses of their own, as the hair being naturally wavy, they save all trouble of dressing the hair which is growing on the head. Weigh- ing less than two ounces, they are not uncomfort- able.

A charming ball dress for a young lady to which I have had my attention called was made of ivory. White satin, trimmed with insertions of gold passe- trienterie, which is studded at intervals with turquoises. The short sleeves are formed of frills of creamy lace, and a tucker of lace appears at the edge of the bodice. The skirt is cut in the latest fashion. With this is worn an opera cloak of turquoise•blue Bilk, lined with cream satin and trimmed with soft fur. Either the dress or cloak can be made in any colour, and in various kinds of silk or other material suitable for evening wear, at extremely moderate prices. The gown also looks Well in black satin, with trimmings of jet or coloured sequin passementerie.

You will perhaps be surprised to hear that d'oyleys of fine netting seem to be coming into fashion again, and they certainly are both pretty and useful. Other fashionable sets are made of Very fine silk canvas ornamented with darning in coloured filoselle, the lines being arranged to form Squares or diamonds, and the doyley finished with a little silk fringe. D'oyleys of gauze or muslin, daintily decorated with flowers executed in a new sort of paint which is said to withstand cleaning, flake charming presents, while 'a much less expen- sive but quite up-to-date set could be made of fine Fhite linen edged with drawn-work or lace Insertion, and embroidered in the centre with a large inital or monogram worked in fine flax or 'washing silk.

Your loving JENNIE.

A meeting of this Council was held in the Council Chamber at the Town Hall, Warrington, on Tuesday evening. £2,000 FROM SIR PE FER WALKER'S FAMILY. The MAYOR said it was no,: often that the Mayor had such a pleasant duty to per- form an he had that night. It was his pleasure to announce and their duty to acknowledge the munificence of the family of the late Sir Andrew Barclay Walker of the gift of £2,000 for public purposes the objects of which he would pr t Bendy read to them. They were no doubt an are that last year they closed an account which was started by a gift from the late Mr. Peter Walker in March 1869 of the sine of £l,OOO which with accrued interest amounted to £1,275. It was their special " memo " fund and good use was male of the money. On examining the account he found that in ad- dition to redeeming a chief rent on the Market Hall and giving the well-deserved gratuity to their lane Chief Constable for his long services, the Corporation altered and improved the Town Hell at a cost of £566 and spent £503 in extensions at the Museum. PROPERTY PURCHASING IN WARRINGTON. TO WHOM SHALL IT BE ENTR.US LTD ? WARM DEBATE.

Alderman SUTTON proposed and Aldaman BIACKRURST seconded th 9 adoption of the,minutes of the Straw; Im- provement Cotnmittee. .

He said he had not given notice of that motion vithout a great dsal of serious consideration. He did not know whether every member of the Council vas aware of the fact that for the past 15 or 18 years the interpretation put uponthe duties of the Street Improvennt Committee in practice by that Council had limited the purchasing of properties by that committee to such properties as trespassed upon a general line of street and were required for the purpose ot malxinit that line uniform from one end to:another.Bat all properties required to be purchased by the Corpora- tion under Parliamentary powers or the pewers given by a provisional order for a general scheme of improvement had by the practice and custom of the Council been delegated to a special committee of the General Purposes Committee, known as the Property Purchasing Sub- Committee. That practice had been found to be on the whole very beneficial to the town. He claimed as a primary condition to the appointment of any man on a committee, whether the Street Improvement or General Purposes, that he should bays no intarest, directly or indirectly, either on his own account or as an agent for any other party, in the sale to the Corporation of any property. (Hear, hear.) Alderman BOLTON : Why didn't you put that in the resolution? •

The MAYOR : No interruption. Let us go on. Alderman ROBERTS (continuine) said he admitted at once that a strict interpretation of the duties of the Street Improvement Committee, had it not 10-ion for the practice and oust.= which had existed so long, would entitle the Street Improvement Committee to claim to have the right to purchase properties required to be pur- chased by the Corporation under Parliamentary power, or under a provisionalorder. The powers necessary had to he acquired by the sub-committee of the General Pur- poses Committee, and be contended that the purchasing of the property ought to be done by the same sub- Committee. Anotmr reason why this Property Pur- chasing Subcommittee ought to deal with the matter was that the Street Improvement Committee was neces- sarily more liable to change in its personnel than the General Purposes Committee, and seeing that some of the schemes of property purchasing extended over a period of years, it was desirable as far as possible that some men should have to do with the carrying out in its entirety of a scheme when it was once entered upon. There had been no objection raised, so far as be knew, to the constitution and power of the General Purposes Committee Sub-Committee until the present time, and he could not understand now, seeing that the committee had done as well as it had, why the objection should be raised, considoing the fact tnat for 18 years the Pro- perty Purchasing Sub-Committee had been carried out so successfully and to a great extent with satisfactim not only to the Council, but to the whole body of the ratepayers. He failed to uaderstand this reason of the objection. Flo did nat want—and he was not going to do unless he was driven—to take up the personal side of the question which he was particularly anxious to avoid.

The effect would be to leave out of the Property Purchasing Committee the et-Mayor, the Chairman of the Finance Committee, and himself. No two men had worked harder or stood out more obstinately against what they be- lieved to be the exorbitant &aims for property in Bridge- street than the ex-Mayor and himself. He did not say they did not get the loyal support of other members on the committee,but they stood out more obstinately than anyone else. WARRINGTON.

Tlin impatience of some A Policy of impatience. politicians is notorious. They want to be making new pro- grammes, revising old ones, changing leaders, and engaging in encounters with opponents, in dis- regard of facts, situations, and the counsels of common sense. An opposition, they say, adopting the Churchill philosophy, "should oppose." It should be always beating drums, waving flags marching about, skirmishing, and dashing its thin battalions against the enemy who is in possession by virtue of his superiority in numbers and dis- cipline. It is not in this way that great political changes come about. It was not in this way that the present DUKE of DEVONSHIRE led the Liberal party after Mr. GLADSTONE ceased to be leader ; following the reverse of 1874, and before he came back again to the fray. It was hardly in this way the historic parties have at any 1 time converted minorities into majorities, The undoing of the work of one General Elec.. tion is always a gradual Process. Sometimes the electors discover their mistake and repent. At other times a steady educational process has to be pursued. Just now what we really see is the former process. The hopes of 1895 were too big and buoyant ever to be realised. The great socia 1 programme which was to supersede an Irish pro- gramme remains very much in the air. Compen" sation is not simple, and it is not general. Old age pensions have not arrived. What we see i 0 that old men are being quietly dropped in maw/ establishments, and regulations are being made which will make getting new situations after fifty a very difficult work indeed. The relief of agricul tural distress has led to new grievances amongs rural ratepayers. Education has once more '

come a keen sectarian and controversial question. In foreign affairs the Government has not beeS scoring at a rapid rate. Lord &Lingual may • be said to have caused interss dia" appointment in the ranks of his supportera' Liberal finance is helping the Government tO meet its heavy and ever-grawing liabilities. Di cussions are educating the public mind. What better results can be obtained by a policy of coal- bat ? Two or three years are still be- tween us and the General Election and they should be utilised by educational work, if it be only of the pep tiva kind. Li country is very impatient of what it has had to suffer lately. Why should we divert its mind from current mat' ters by saying what is to be done in four years' time? A little patience will yield a good. crop. "Time is on our side," as Mr. GransroNE once said. We lose nothing by being even-tempered, patient, and in no hurry to crystalise our ideas into programmes and points. There will be another reaction, and when it comes it will bear its own programme with it. The programme will not make the swing; it is the swing that will bring the programme.

PRESIDENT MCKINLEY shows A merica'3 Future. keen appreciation of the &fa' unities of the situation. 11( does not want to be too rigid in circumscribing

limits within which the new acquisitions are to t, governed or protected. The new colonial polie9 is to be allowed to grow in accordance with the necessities and the opportunities of the case. Til° relations between Washington and the ex-Spanish islands are not set forth in his Message, as had been expected. Time is to be allowed for think' ing them out. Many things may occur to sugge4 changes. The vague nature of the origin of tbe war will colour its results for some time to

It cannot b e ascertained whether the experiment- which are being made will answer. The Philippll°6 may be quite incapable of governing themselVeSl and the Cubans may prefer to fight amongsV themselves rather than to make the best use of the) freedom they have obtained. Much must depoo4 upon circumstances, and they have yet to nle°

themselves. But some elements in the situat are distinct enough. The war-tax is tO continued. It has yielded well, it has 0" been an inconvenience, and it will have to be CO tinned. America must pay for the change which has taken place in her aims and relations. Slie must build up a Navy, and take rank with the other powers aspiring to have partial command a the sea. She has an immense seaboard to proteao and a good ninny islands have now been added t, the responsibilities of her position. The shir yards are busy. A British firm has undertake° to make guns for the American Navy, and 1134 purchased an establishment in Virginia to Lula"' take the work on the terms of the contract. Ole American Navy will, in two or three years' tinteo be equal to that of Germany or Italy in nucaber°' and superior to either in effective fighting power' The next thing to observe is that the aria must grow also. Instead of a paltry 2500 Xll°l3' President Moliticr,EY suggests a peace esta.blish: ment of 100,000 mien. What this means . penditure will soda be obvious. Barracksexerose ) grounds, ranges, arms, staff, and annual s for wages and food will entail heavy exPendlt:llll There is no prospect of any reduction of taatt

===== 0002601_18981209_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
in the United States for some time to come, and probably no charge will be made in the tariff. How these enlargements will affect American poli- tics we cannot say ; but they will not make it any lighter for the general taxpayer, who usually has to step in to pay for glory at a considerable rate. The conditions of life in the United States cannot change very materially in a few years ; but they are entering upon new conditions in that sturdy and enterprising Republic. The ring-fence politics will go, and the room for the play of Imperialistic feeling will be very considerable. Is the Repub- lican to be known in future as the Imperial party? Or will the Democrats prefer to declare for Colon- ialism? The two parties have not been readily distinguishable in the past ; but the Democrats have leant towards Free-trade. What isto be the fate of Free-trade now ? Answers to these ques- tions will come in time.

The eminent French Admiral who proposed that France should sanction privateering in her next naval war with England finds an unexpectedly large number of supporters, who are writing to the French papers, full of enthusiasm, about it. It is surprising that not one of these correspondents should understand or mention the loss of dignity and respect which France would suffer by a course of action which would be bound to be as useless now as it was when the slow-sailing merchantmen of the Napoleonic era out-sailed the very vessels designed to capture them. In having recourse to privateering France would fall behind even Spain. That such a proposal should be entertained by serious people shows how far France has fallen— not from her ideals, for she never reached them, but from the standard of civilisation laboriously attained in the inteivals of her internecine feuds and envies. The Dreyfus case has exposed appall- ing weaknesses in French character. No subter- fuge is too mean, no cruelty too cruel, so long as its use will save the situation. It has not saved the situation in the Dreyfus case, and still deeper depths of ignominy and dishonour appear to have been resorted to in order to accomplish that object. Baffled in war, it is evident that France would resort to tactics just as unscrupulous as her lead- ing soldiers use to-day in times of peace. Treaties would be weak as ropes of sand. It does not matter much to anyone outside France, perhaps, but the spectacle of a nation seeking safety in dis- honour is always a sad one, and teaches many painful lessons.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

--- RUGBY FOOTBALL. Neither of the teams representing the Warrington Football Club had a league fixture On Saturday. rho seniors went to Barrow to play the return friendly match with the club of that town, and succeeded in scoring a goal and four triAs to their opponents nothing. J. T. Bate, the Warrington captain, accompanied the team once more and his presence had much to do will the victory. He was the first to score, and his partner, Ashurst, credited himself with a couple more tries, while Harris notched the fourth. Barrow were not very strongly npresented so that the victory cannot be considered a performance of any im- portance. Warrington played very well, though the paving among the backs was not of the sort one ex- pects from a first division team. The weather was very wet, and this bad something to do with the poor hand- ling of the ball.

In the forward department, the " A "team youths effected an improvement, and all tlinur,h the game were more than a match for the heavier Barrovians. Bate and Ashurst were in grand form, the skipper seeming to have benefitted by his res.:. Harris and Hof- croft worked well together, and are in consequence chosen in the same positions this week. Cross was only good on the defence, and is dropped again, Isherwood returning to the centre with Fish on the wing. Hallam played a good game and EA2M3 to be getting off his habit of following up when there is no need.

The match to-day with Wigan is the first of four suc- cessive home matches that Warrington have in hand. They ought to win the lot. Wigan, Stockport, Birken- head Wanderers, and Rune rn are not playing in form which should give them much chance of defeating the Warringtonians at Wilderepool. The homeiters must not be satisfied with less than 6 points out of these three League engagements. Wigan always give War- rington a gocd game, and the contest to-day should be well worth seeing. Warrington will have to play their hardest to hold their own in the scrimmages.

RAILWAY ACCIDENT AT FRODSHA.III

STATION.

NARROW ESCAPE OF A RAILWAY PORTER. About 4-30 on Wednesday afternoon an accident of a peculiar and alarming nature occurred at the frodsbam Railway Station which resulted in seri- ous damage being done to the signal box and the railway siding, butt happily was not attended with any loss of life. It appears that a goods train was standing in the siding preparatory to leaving for Chester, and had steam up, when the pointsman dropped the signal for the Manchester express to go through to Chester. The driver of the goods train evidently real the signal as for himself and started his engine, with the resu:t —as the points were not set to take him on ta the main line—that be ran his engine into the signal-box, which is only a little distance off the " stop-block " which terminates the siding. Considerable damage was done to the signalling levers and connecting rods,rendering them unserviceable. Luckily, how- ever, the telegraphic instruments remained capable of doing their work, and information of the acci- dent and a request for a breakdown gang was at once sent on to Chester, and shortly after the gang were busily at work, there being hardly any inter- ference with the traffic, The signal box was con- siderably damaged, the top part being lifted bodily out of position, and the lower brickwork, which also constitutes a storeroom for lamps, &c., suffered very materially. At the time of•the accident there was no one in the storeroom, but a few seconds be- fore the goods train ran into it one of the station porters had been busily engaged inside. The signal- men seeing that something was wrong left the signal box, and possibly saved his life by doing se. The traffic was carried on, as mentioned with scarcely any perceptible suspension, and until the " block" is repaired and the signals made workable the section will be worked with flagmen. Had the points been set for the main line and the express thundered up behind, before a collision could be avoided, a terrible disaster would have occurred.

THE EXAMINER FRIRAY, DFCEMBER 9, 198

RUNCORN NOTES.

[BY A CORELESPONDENTI Has the last been heard of the School Board Elec- tion? Far from it ! and it is interesting to note that—though some people may think it smatters somewhat of " locking the stable door after the horse has gone "—the " progressives " are g3ing to profit by their experience, and from the present up to the next School Board Election will be employed in placing the question of education fairly and squarely before the electorate. Thera are to be no half measures. There are to be no more compromises. Steady and efficient work is to ba and I feel sure will be accomplif,ed,so that the triumph of the pro- gressives though long delayed will ultimately be the more glorious.

Yet, though this is the intention of the progres- sive party; it must n)t for one moment be supposed that they era going to act the part of "Tnorn-in-the flesh" and make the administration of the deno- minationalists mms difficult of execution. Lr,t me disabuse the minds of the people of that idea once for all. They will, as they always have done, serve the ratepayers consistently and worthily ; and in addition they will see that their opponents —if they do hold the majority for a little while— commit no indiscretions. In thl4 way four sound, strong repre:entatives can accomplish much.

The Chairmanship will of course be a question for the first meeting of the new Board, and the name of the Rev. Canon Wood has already fallen from the lips of one if not more of the " denomina- tionalists "as the member who should occupy the seat. Personally I differ from that opinion, and as I attribute a large amount of the sterling work which the Board has accomplished in the past to be due to the old, tried, and valued services of Mr. James Pritchard, a gentleman who has o3cupied the position of chairman of the Board with honour for so long a period, I venture to think his services command no small title to the post of honour.

Halton Cemetery called for a little consideration, and formed the subject of a lengthy discussioa at the Parish Council meeting held on Tuesday. The Council decided to have a look roun I, and it is very evident from the strength of the o,rinioas expreqsed by the Councillors present at t meeting that something will be done. Tnere wa3 au )her matter that troubled the" city fathers" of Llaii-on some- what—the St. Thotuas• Cnarity—and not feeling quite clear on the point the Council wisely decided to trouble the powers theft be at the Charity Com- missioners Oflice,with just a few questions. The result is being awaited with iraterest.

The Runcorn Shorthand Writers' Association fills a loag felt want in the town, and numbers of aspir- ants in the art of Paoaography are flocking roan 1, giving to the promoters most encour3.ging si.ip24rt. It is understood that the moath of December will be devoted to practice, and readings will be given at the Society's rooms. Savage's B-idge, at speeds varying from 30 to 120 wards per minute. This is not all the Society offers, as the literary benefits, in the shape of sound literature, to say nothing of a course ot ugeful lectures, are also consp:cuous on Ithe projected programme.

RUNCORN SCHOOL BOARD.

The monthly meeting was held at the Town Flail on Tuesday night. Mr. J. Pritc lard presided and there were present—the itev. Canon Maitland Wood. Rev. H. B. Gore, Mr. E. Imisoa, Mr. F. J. Norman, J.P., and J. W. Lightburu (clerk). THE TEACHING STAFF. . .

The School Management Committee reported that applications ts be continued as assistant teachers had been received from Nellie McDonald, Edith M.2Lean, E. A. Carr and A. Dyer, and the Committee recommended that tney be engaged in the capacity named at LI.O per annum each. It was further recommended that appli- cation be made to the Eiucation Department for sauc- tioo t 3 borrow an adoitional sum o, £ll5 in connec:ion with the extensi.m of Victoria-road Sc.mol. The minutes wire approved on the motion of Mr. Norman, seconded by Mr. unison.

, SCHOOL BOARD 3 AND SECONDARY EDUCATION. The Clerk submitted the agenda of a for:hcoming meeting of the Association of School Boards, at which resolutions will be submiteed with regard to secondary education. The Rev. Canon Wood moved "That it is desirable,in the opinion of this Board, that in the constitution of authorities for secondary education, School Boards should be represented." As everyone was aware, re- marked Canon Wood, we were advancing with a time when secondary eiucation would be put upon a more regular and improved footing. What the Bill was which the Government were about to introduce was not known ; but the Association of Schcol Boards was very strongly of opinion that whatever be the constitution of the local authorities for secondary education, School B iards should be represented upon that authority. In secondary education it was clear that primary educa- tion was interested because the whole drif,, of modern legislation would be ootrely c intradicted if the scholars in elementary scaools were no promoted wnen they hai shown themselves capable,to secondary Echools. It coukl hardly be doubted tnerefore that it was necessary that the education given in secondary Pchaols should haee in some way a connection with that given in primary sohools, and it was most desirable that the authorities of both primary arid sec-ndary edu- cation should see that the whole course of education was of such a character that chi iren advanced from the elementary schools might nor, find themselves in an unfair position when they reached the secondary schools. The whole curriculum from primary schools to secondary schools should be on a plan, and that plan not dislocated in the middle. Those in chwge of primary education should be able to eicroress the:r opinions, aied those who ought to carry weight with those in charge of secondary education as to what that place ought to bo. Woodier (clerk).—A. letter was read from Sir Richard Brooke calling attention to the untidy state of the Halton Cemetery, the walks and grass of which were sadly neglected.—The Chairman said the question had been a sore one ever since they took over the burial hoard'.4 duties.—A long discussion followed in which the Clerk exnlained that when the sexton's salary was raised from £5 to £7 it was mentioned that he should keep the cemetery in nice order. CHRISTMAS MAGAZINES, NEW BOOKS, ETC.

"HOLLY LEAVES."—The Christmas number of the" Illustrated Sporting and Dramatic News " " Holly Leaves "—is one of the brightest and most seasonable of the Yuletide magazines. Its crimson and gold cover is alone sufficient to make it attractive to the purchaser, while its contents never belie their reputation for excellence. This year there are as many full-page pictures in the number as there are short stories, while minor illustrations abound. Among the contributions to the literary contents of the number are: " A Sensation Fare," by F. W. Robinson; " The Mystery of Groot Adrian's Poort," by H. A. Bryden ; " The Plunge of Janders,' by H. G. Harper ; " The Bally slop Murder," by Bertram H. Harrison ; and "A Skeleton Force," by Hope Eric. The stories are full of interest, and are of the style likely to be popular with readers of "The Spotting and Dramatic News." There are incidents bordering on the supernatural, humorous sketches, and sporting anecdotes in profusion. In accardance with ttedir usual custom the proprietors of "Holly Leaves" have given away a coloured supplement, the subject this year being a capitally executed reproduction of IV. H. Overend's famous picture " The Hero of Trafalgar," "THE WINDSOR MAGAZINE."—We had been led to expect much by the off-repeated announcements concerning the Christmas number of the" Windsor Magazine," and now that the double number of this popular montely is on the bookstalls we are ready to admit that it Comes up to expectations. First and foremost in the attractions of the issue is the gratis copy of " Phra the Phoenician," by Edwin Lester Arnold, than which a mere entrancing ro- mance could not have been selected. We must say that we were slightly disapoointed when we saw the title of the novel, ler in previous years the supple- ment to the ' Windsor " has been a story specially written,and therefore all the ieoreattractive.Still the fact that we had read Mr. Arnold's cleverly con- ceived work before will not make it less prized by those to whom it is new. A number of excellent illustrations from the pen of Li. M.Paget lend addi- tional charm to the pages of the romance. We have only one fault to And with the "Windsor Magazine" itself, in this issue, and that is with regard to the coloured inks used in printing about a quarter of the pages. In our eyes this is no enhancement, but rattier a detraction, for several of the pictures are rendered indistinct by the use of unsuitable tints. But the literary portion of the number com- pensates for any fault such as we have mentioned. Rudyard Kipling contributes a school-boy story, entitled " Stalky and Co," in his own peculiar and inimitable style, the perusal of which makes one feel glad that it is the first of a series from Mr. Kipling's pen. Carkran, Beetle, and Maack are three delightfully mischievous school boys, whose pranks are of a nature to entertain both young and old. Mr. S. R. Crockett is the author of a serial story "Joan of the Sword," which opens this month and gives promise of being exceptionally interesting. The scene is pitched in some imaginary kingdom equi-distant alike from Austria, Germany, and Denmark, while the times are those of the battle- axe, the sword, and the tournament. Joan of the Sword is a heroine et unusual spirit, a fitting suc- cessor to Joan of Arc. Mr. Frank Richards illus- trates the story in a fashion rather florid, but on that account quite in keeping with the tone of the narrative. 'Stories of the Gold Star Line" by those clever collaborateurs L.T. Meade and Robert Eustace, is a series of adventures related by George Conway, purser of the "Morning Star." The first is entitled "The Jewelled Cobra," an account of a very auda- cious and skilful theft being just as skilfully pre- vented. Marie Corelli, Conan Doyle, Bret Harte,and L. T. Meade are among the other contributors to the Christmas number of the " Windsor Magazine." THE "STRAND MAGAzINE."—The Christmas double number of "The Strand Magazine" is an amazing shillingworth, containing as it does 208 pages, aad 211 illustrations. Dr. Conae Doyle tells "'The Story of the Brezlian Cat" in thrilling lan- guage, bicluey Paget illustrating the more striking incidents of this exciting story with great skill and effectiveness. This is the first item in the contents of the number, which are too numerous for us to detail. After Dr. Doyle's blood-curdling narrative we turn with pleasure to " The Madness of Mr. Lister," by W. W. Jacobs, a story brim- full of comical conversation and possessing a most laughable denouement. Mr. Jacobs is already one of the most popular writers of the present day, and has won a high re- putation with his wittily-written sketches of life in the coasting trade. L. T. Meade and Robert Eustace write one of those enthralling tales based on some mysterious scientific theory for which they have gained wide popularity. Its title is "Where the Air Quivered." Other excellent items are " The Hour-Glues," by Rebert Barr; " The Adventure of the Cross-eyed Q C."by Grant Allen; "Second Class," by G. H. 'Robins; "The Larrikin of Diamond Creek" by E. W. Hornung; and" The Looting of the Con- voy," by Walter Wood. There are several special articles of wide interest, and an interview with the latest hero of cycling, Mr. John Foster Fraser. Taken altogether the current number of " The Strand" is one of the best budgets of fiction and fact Messrs. Newnes have ever turned out. " THE WIDE WoRLD MAGAzINE."—Louis de Rougemont's incredible, but none the less interest- ing adventures take up a large portion of the space in the Christmas is dte of the above monthly maga- zine, which is double its ordinary size, and contains double the quantity of"truth stranger than fiction," Numerically speaking there are 110 less than 11 articles, and 17 stories of adventure. The most not- able of the latter is "The voyages of the Mole Hill," by T. Webster Smith. The publishers in a note say they have in their possession the real name of the ship and all papers and documents bearing upon it. Apparently they are not the only people who have ' these particulars, for, since the publication of the story an action ler libel has been brought against Messrs. Newnes end Co., which has cost them a little over £6OO. It is rather curious that the plain- tiffs name was "Cost." In the light of tile action "The voyages of the Mole Bill "should be read with mare than ordinary interest. "WELDON'S LADIES' JOURNAL" CHRISTMAS NUM- BAR is a perfect treasure-house in the way of fem- inine adornment. To the anxious and harrassed ones seeking for a pretty idea for an evening toilette, its fashion pages afford infinite variety and lunbounded scope in the lovely combiaaticns and 'effective styles there represented. Some new ideas in winter millinery are shown and should prove very acceptable just BOW. The" crowning glory " is elso well looked after in the hairdressing lesson for this montb,as the style of coiffure is particularly becoming and easy of arrangement. To those in- terested in church decoration some valuable hints are given in the article on this subject. With the menu of a Cnristmas dinner, to which the cookery department seasonably devotes some space, the complete method of preparing each comestible is fully detailed. The Boudoir Supplement contains an extremely interesting ghost stery, and a paner pattern of a pretty costume, aaaptable for either day or evening wear, is presented, together ,vith a large coloured plate of fashions. This excellent budget is further supplemented by two chromo plates entitled "Divided Attention," by A. J. Elsley, and "Sporting Prospects," by Fannie Moody. TIM LkDY."—lt, is a matter productive of no little wonder that the proprietors of "Tire Lady " Can issue a Christmas number of such immense pro- portions for 61, and at the same time present to all purchasers a pretty picture of child-life, in colours, called " The Little Peacemaker? There is any quantity of pictures of the latest fashion models scattered through the number, all with des- criptive paragraphs handy, and no scarcity of light reading. The fair sex cannot help but be delighted with the multitudinous features of interest in the Cnristmes number of" The Lady." " CASSELL'S MAGAZINE" comes before the public with a Christmas number second to none in literary and pict irial value. The presentation plate is a large phot )gravure reproduction of Nlis klacgregor's famous picture, shown at this year's academy, "The boom with the Secret Door." it is reminiscent of the terrible days of the civil war, and produced in a size suitable fdr framing. Mr. Max Pemberton's new serial "The Garden of Swords " commences in the namber, and as far as we are able to judge from the opening chapters it cannot fail to fascinate the I vast body of admirers of the author of " The Im- pregnable City," "Kronstadt," and other remarkable works. His new story is one which deals with the great siege of Strasburg during the France-German war of 1670,and is replete with stirring and drama- tic scenes. Mr. Stanley L. Wood is entrusted with the illustration of the novel,and the work could not be in more capable hands. The catalogue of authors who have combined to make the pages of the mag- azine so obsorbingly interesting is an imposing one and includes such names as Ouida, Anthony Hope, Robert Barr. William le Queux, Guy Booth by,Halli- well Sutcliffe, and L. T. Meade. Mrs. Meade' story, written in collaborotion with Mr. Robert Eustace, is one cf weird interest, aud embodies ii mystery for

the solution of which ten prizes are offered. The special articles include " How her Majty and the Royal Family spend Christmas," " Madame Sarah Fernhardt," "The Guards of Europe,"" Heroes of all Nations," and many others which will appeal to al- most every glass of reader. THE SUICIDE OF MR. P. PETERS.

On Friday evening the deputy-coroner of Wigan held an inquest relative to the death of Mr. Peter Peters, for- merly statimmaster at Wigan, who committed suicide on Wednesday evening. Mr. H. Milligan, in opening the inquiry, said the jury had a v:try painful duty be- fore them, the deceased gentleman being known person. ally to most of them. Ile felt sure he was expressing the feeling of everyone present when he said how much nest regretted the cause of this inquiry.—Sarah Peters stated that she was the wife of the deceased, who was forty-three years of age, and up to the time that he was paid off in September last he was stationmaster at Wigan, in the employ of the London and North-Western Railway Company. He had been very low-spirited lately. On Wcdneslay evening he came from his bed- room twice, and he told witness to lock up a eeeque which he took from his pocket. The second time that he came down the door of the kitchen closed so that witness could not follow him. Upon going out of the front door she put a boy over their wall, who unlocked the door. Witness found the deceased by the coal heap. Be was in a reclining position. Witness put her apron round him, and he walked into the kitchen.—By the Coroner : He did net say anything when going out. Witness did tact ace him take any- thing with him.—She did not notice that he was bleeiiug.—This was all the cvidecc 3 taken. —Mr. James Wilson, solicitor, who apotared on behalf of the family, said there was no doubt. that Mr. Peters' EC 7craCC9 of his connection with the railway company, t whom he had beau a faithful ssrvant for twenty-six years, had been most depressing, and on this day, when not quite master of himself, he had been led to this act. It was a matter of extreme sorrow to them when they found that their efforts fer his reinstate- ment in the cmmany's service were not successful. On behalf of the family he wished to express the,r thanks for the sympathy which had been extended by the Coroner and all concerned in the inquiry.—The Coroner again referred to the deep sympathy they felt for the family.—After a short deliberation, the jury returned a verd ct of" Suicide whilst temporarily insane."

A WARRINGTON ELECTRICIAN ANT) TIM APPRENTICE. At the Warrington Borough Po:ice Court on Fri,lay before the Mayor (in the chair) Mr. James Heph-rd,Dr. Gornall and Councillor Smethurst, George Platt, of Martinscroft, apprentice of Mr. Walter Morton, elec- trician, St. Jarnes'•strset was summoned for not obeying the magistrates' order to return to his work.—Mr. A. Browne appeared for toe complainant and Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins for the defendant.—Mr. Browne explained that an order had been made by the magistrates for the defendant to return to his work and he had not done 50, ignoring their order to the inconvenience of his client, Mr. Morton.—Mr. Jenkins said be was glad to see the earns magistrates on the bench as on the 21st October, when an order was made for his elient to return to his work, and a reconamnadation that fresh Indentures be made. He was sorry to say that shortly after that, his client callod at his office and said it was not his intention to sign any fresh indentures. Be was quite determined not to go tack to work for Mr. Morton. Be reasoned with him bat to no purpose. His client seemed to have got in such a nervous state over it that it was cpite fu'ile to expect it. He said he would go to prison in preference to working fer Mr. Morton. lie sincerely trusted that some means could be devird to bring Platt and his master together. If Mr. Morton would make some advance.—Mr. Browne: That would be rather infra dig. for his master.—Mr. Jenkins : Well I sun, at then that the young man should approach his master.— Mr. Browne: Platt is very obstinate. Mr. Browne said Mr. Morton had had some very important work and had lost a Fret deal by Platt not corning back. He thought he would learn better sense if he was sent to gaol.—Mr. Jenkins said he would ask the Bench to take into con- sideration the young man's nervous development. I: was admitted that Mr. Morton sass Got by any means a pattern of a mater.—Mr. Browne stated that Platt had eighteen months to serve before he was 21. Eventually Platt decided to return with Mr. Mortoa to work. Mr. Browne stated that they would c arty out their promise made on the 21st October,to give him increased pay,and the Bench adjourned the case for 14 days for matters to be settled.

UNHAPPY MARITAL RELATIONS AT WARRINGTON.— RELIGIOUS DIFFERENCES BETWEEN HUSBAND AND WIFE. At the Warrington Borough Police Court on Friday before the Mayor (in the chair) and other magistrates, Joseph Rigby Vernon was charged with committing an aggravated assault upon his wife El zabeth Vernon on the 28th November.—Defendant pleaded not guilty.— Mr. A. Browne appeared for the complainant and Mr. F. R. Roberts represented the defenclant.—Mr.Browne sail he appeared in the case very regretfully as he had known both parties all his life. They had tried to come te terms but could not do so, and it was their last resort to bring the matter before the court.—Mr. Robarts said a great deal of the trouble had been cau-ei by a religioua difficulty. When Mr. Vernon married his wife she was Converted to the Roman Catholic Church. But his client complained Mrs. Vernon had not carried out that arrange- ment properly as some of the children were not brought sip in the Roman Catholic religion. and according to the Roman Cathelic Church, it is an unforgiveable Sin to allow one's children to be brought up in an Win -re- ligion.—After further endeavouring to settle the disputed points, it was decided to let the magic- trates adjudicate.—Mr Browne in opening his 1 case said defendant and his wife had lived a most miserable life for some time. He drank, and on the morning in question she was having a cup of tea and he took it away from her. He pullai the table away from her and after some words he struck her, giving her a black eye. He called her most filthy names and thumped her about the head and said he would thump her where DO marks would be seen,and did so. It was not the first offence, they must consider it was an a2gravated one, and complainant had decided to apply also for a separa- tion order.—Elizabath Vernon bore out her solictot'a Statement. She had:been married 13 years and resided at School Brow. She had four ehildren, (three girls and one boy).—Mrs. Barkbouse, a neighbour, gave corrobora- tive evidence.—Mr. Rebuts said the matter was chiefly a religions difficulty.. The defendant had said strong things and so had lal3 wife. He thought one was as bad SA the other. The action was Simply a peg on which to hang an application for separation. But his client also desired a scparation.—Joseph Rigby Vernon denied the assault on his wife. He had never lifted his fist to her. She had struck him timei Without number, and held a carving knife to his throat. —Alice Vernon (16) daughter of defendant. also gave evidence.—The Beq,ch considered there had been an aggravated assault and fined defendant 103. and costs. The complainant than appiied for a separation order. Evidence was given and the Bmeh made an order on defendant for 10.4. a weak and the wife to take the custody of the children.

DIPORTANT NORTHERN UNION MEETING.

SEVERE PENALTIES.

A meeting of the Gets rd Committee of the Northern Union was held in Manchester on Tuesday evening, Mr. D. F. Burnley (Bitley) presiding. Owing to the impor- tant items oa the agenda more than ordinary interest was centred in the proceedings. The Swintoe club appealed against the deduction of two points by the Lancashire Senior Competition Committee, who in- flicted the penalty in accordance with the bona fide-om- ployment rule. After brief consideration trio committee I upheld the decision of tho Lancashire Executive.—The question of referees' fees was then discussed. It was suggested that in county matches and where referees officiated outside their own counties its games in which one or more senior competition clubs were engaged,or in cup ties, the fee should be one guinea. The suggestion was adopted.--Tee committee of the Lancashire Senior Competition asked for a ruling as to whether the fact of one club offering another . club a consideration for the transfer of an amateur affected the status of such player. The reply was in the negative. The point aiesas out of the registration of Fletcher, of Seaton,by Oldham Kingston Rovers appealed for a reductioa of the fine of £5O recently inflicted upon them, but the Co mmittee decided not to entertain the application.—Kendal Bornets,Gleeby Hornets(Oldham), Seaton, and Caraforth were a dmitt?ci to membership of the Union.—An important matter ar icing out of the sus- 'pension of Fitzgerald the Batley three-quarter, was next dealt with. It was alleged that the club had played Fitzgerald during a period when be was not in a bona- fide employment. The case had previously come before the special sub-committee autherised to dee, with sus- pected cases, and Fitzgerald was provisionally suspended on Friday last. The offending club had now legal assist ance and, in addition to the principal officials, Fitz- gerald and a number of witnesses were in attendance. After a long discussion the Secretary informed the press representatives that the Committee, after a careful con- sideration of the case,had decided to suspend Fitzgerald until May I. 1900. Further, the Committee were of opinion that the Batley officials ought to have satisfied themselves as to the bona:fides of Fitzgerald's employ- ment, and their failure to do eo was a breach of the same bye-law. The club would be fined £6o.—The re- a:seising business was not of public interest.
