As the theft was cmnmitt,ed within 500 yards from tin borough boundary, the court had jurls.liztion iu the case.

The prisoner was further charged with stealing two geese, of tie vatun of 103 , tan wow.] ty of Mr. Robert Curran, architint, Grappenhall, . .

The Chief Constable stated that the geese were last seen in Mr. Curran's yird at two o'clock on the after- neon of the 15th November. The same a' tOtnoon they were missed. At a quarter to five a gardener named Thomas Bleese, of Latctiford, saw the prisoner in Hunt's-lane. Hill Top, coming from the direo'ion of Mr. Curran'S house. He had a hamper on his shoulders, and it was covered with a sack. It should be explained that a hamper had been missed, as well as the geese. Previous to the robbery the prisoner went to Mrs. Rebecca Looker in the market and told her that he had nine geese,for sale,and asked her if she would buy any. She replied that it all depended upon whether the geese wese suitable as to size. Th.) prisoner went away, but later on returned to Mrs. Looker, wao was a poultry dealer, /kn., carrying on business in the market, and offere I two geese for sale, and she bought them giving 4s. 11d. each. Although Mrs. Locker could not swear that tee prisoner was the man who sold the geese she could say that he was certainly the one who made the arrangement for the sale. However, there was a man I named William Longshaw, greengrocer, and nephew to Mrs. Locker, who could prove that the prisoner was the man who came to see Mrs. Looker, so that the identiti• cation was complete. The prisoner, who made no reply to the charge, was committed to the Liverpool Quarter Sessions. These will be held en the 1311 Dec,mber.

THF REPRESENTATION OF FAIRFIELD WARD. Sir,—l was extremely glad to see that Mr. Duncan had lo?.en returned again, and by such a good majority. Craik, whom I am sure would please alt parties, and would be an excellent representative, but who declines to be the puppet of any party. I am a Conservattve, but I am not goinl to vote for a man who is thrust on us, because he happens to have money, neither am I going to vote for a man to represent us, who lives at Newton. I think in Fairfield Ward, we can find men amongst U 3, quite capable of lookinz after our interests, without import- ing one, either from Wmwick, or Newton.-I(aurs truly, A WORKINGMAN.

THE PROPO3ED NEW HOTEL IN ORFORD.

Sir,—As a resident in and off Orford-lane for 30 years I cannot conceive why myself and a many others who take a little ref reshment,should be compelled to go froml the bottom of Orford-lane to the nearest place,that is the ' Lord Rodney, to get a glass of Peter Walker and Sons'l spirits or ale, which we prefer to any other. I think your readers will agree with me that there is a house in that locality which belongs to the above firm that will make a splendid hotel with a bowling greeo,which would the very convenient for recreation and social amusement. Moreover we find Pater Walker and Son's public houses are being conducted, second to none, both in cleanliness and soberness and in addition to that there is n credit given, and no games of rings or dominoes allowed to en- 1 courage custom and drunkenness. Sir, the above pure I facts should speak volumes in favour of our having iltalker's refreshments nearer to our homes as we have as I moth right to have our beer and spirits as a teetetaller.l to have tea or coffee. And one firm has no more rig'at to monopolize than another.—Yours December Ist, 1898.

EQUAL JUSTICE.

THE GUARDIANS AND THE LA.DIES' VISITING

CO NI MITTE O.

Sir,—l read in your Saturday's isme the report of the meeting of the Board of Guardians on 23rd ult. I came to the conclusion that a more shameful proceeding I could scarcely believe any public buly being capable of transactini!. That the ex parte statement; a a re- cently appointed official, extorted in confidence, should be publicly discussed, and not in committee, and ac lowed to go forth to the public prev, casting aspersion on that most estimable body th 3 Ladies Visit- ing Committee ar.d on the efficient and kind hearted Matron, surpasses my understanding. In fact it is my oplnion that the Clerk would have been quite justified In ruling the discussion out of order. I would be the last man to stifle enquiry into any abuse of our pablic Institutions but in the nom' of Heaven let it be fair and not one-sided. "Audi alterant partent " hear the other aide, before exposing any one, particularly ladiea, to false impressions. Again, the Guardians allowing the names of the ladiea recommended to be added to the visiting committee, but declined by them, to go to the public press was a curse wanting in fine feelings and delicacy to those ladies. • . .

I must ex-press my admiration for the two gentlemen, viz., the Rev. W. El. Drummond anl Mr. A. J. Jolley, vrho protested aqainst these unjust proa?elings. I am, dear sir, yourstruly,

FAIRPLIY.

TH3 BAND OF HOPE_ METING.

Sir,—ln your report ef tnis meeting; in th 4 " Examiner" of the 26:h of November, Vlore are two points upon which, with your kind permission, I will make a few observations.

---- - The phenornonon of Mr. R. A. Naylor blessing the publicans is the first ens. The fact that Mr. Naylor is the Chairmen of an oreanisation which seeks to train up children and young persons to avoid the public-house, may fairly be taken as a proof that Mr. Naylor regard; the drink traffic as an evil thing. And yet he goes out of his way to give a good testimonial to the men and women who are engaged in it. I confess I cannot see the logic of this. If it be true that a man's character is more or less influenced by his occupation and surround- ings, then surely a trade which protluces drunkenness, crime, and destitution, cannot fail to have an evil effect upon all who live and grow rich by it. There ' is something so hard and Gruel in taking the money of Door people for drinks which only make them more wretched still that I feel constrained to think that there must be a lack of human kindness and sympathy in tee traders. Still I would not send them to " the bottom- less pit" to which Mr. Naytor referred, bat on the con- trary, my worst wish for them is that they may be brought to see that it is not rieht, even for the sake of wealth,to carry on a trade which in the words of a great brewer "is only another development of the war be- tween Heaven and Hell." My second point is the blessing of " the trade " itseq by no less an authority than a professional preacher,Mr. Gambit; Walker. Mr. Walker is reported to have said that " he behaved with the chairman that they had no right to attack brewers or publicans tor the simple reason that they were carrying on a perfectly legiti• mate business that was sanctioned by the Sale." Unfortunately, it is only too true that the Liquor traffic is a government institution. The imprimatur of Parlia- ment, however is far from being a true test that the measures it puts forth are sound and good, and bene- ficial to the people. Mr. Walker knows, or ought to know, that the Legislature which sanctions and encour- ages the sale of intoxicating liquors in England in the interests of the exchequer and the publicans, is the very earns that legalisee vice of a revolting charalt3r in India in the interest of the Army! The divine axiom, " The tree is known by its fruit," applies with peculitr force to rhe liquor tree in this country. A tree whose fruit is a terrible crop of drunkenness, crime and destitution, is hardly one to be blessed by a man who regards himself as a minister if Him who ttueht his distiples to pray, " Load us not into temptation." Then again, if the drink traffic is a legitimate and good ono why have recourse to Bands of Hope, good teal- plary and other teetotal devices to do battle against it ? I never heir of oreanisations got up against the ns ,nu- facturerS of doors and window frames,or against bikers, grocers, and tailors, for the very obvious reason that all these men are useful members of semi oty,and minister to the necessities and com!orts of hum In life. But it is far otherwise with the trade of the publican. am liquids simply no net vat wants, but create false appatites whose indulgence leads to shame, ill-health, poverty, and, in numberless c to de tth itself.—Yours truly, lONA.

OUR LOCAL CLIUR.CTIES.

WYCLIFFE P.S.A. SOCIE FY-OPEN MEETING.

T.her-e-was a large at'endance ia-Wycliffe Church on Sunday afternoon when ac open meeting in connection with the P.S. A. Society was held. The service was pre- sided over by Mr. G. Thompson, of Great Sankey, In the course of a brief speelh, Mr. Thompson alluded to the temptations which beset young men in the present day. He severely deprecated the gambling craze in con- nection with sports and recreations, acri advised young men to give no countenance to gambling. Addressing himself to the married men preelt he expree led the opinion that they were getting a little bit selfish. In taking their pleasures it was getting the custom of mee to leave their wives a; home. It was their duty to try to make life brighter for those who had to undertake the drudgery of the home during the week. The speaker then proceeded to refer to the formation of a sick club in cennection with the P.S.,C. Society, and hoped that it would be taken up by the members and prove a success- ful venture. In the absence of Mr. J. Walley, of Man- ebeser, through illness, the address was delivered by Mr. Alfred Carney, of Warrington. He lotrodueed his remarks by referring to the life of Pdiriam, the sister of Moses, and stated that it was woman who inspired man to noble actions. Men needed the help of woman to en- courage and to inspire. A woman's sphere in life was no, bounded by the four walls of her home. There were great social evils at the present day which would never be solved unless they were solved by the women. 911 history was with him when he sail that all social reforms had come from women. He illustrated hie meaning by raferring to the work of Harriet Beecher Stowe, Elizabeth FIT, Florence Nightingale, and Catherine Booth. Women had also a sphere amongst the nations of the earth. There was one great national cause at the pr2sant time which should be dear to the heart of every WOaltn. A woman knew the horrors ot war ; the carnage of the battlefield filled hr with loathing. She sent her busband and sons to be shot down like stuhle in the fieirl. The speaker referred ti the Czar's rescript, and urged the women to usetheir influence for a better understanding amongst the nations on the question of war and arma- ments. In conclusion Mr. Comes, referred to the influ- ence which women should have .in Christ's Church.— The soloist was Mr. W. H. Ireland who +lave an exlenent interpretatim of the solos : "The Star of Be.thiehem " and "The Light of the World." Mr. Payton presided at the orean with hie usual ability.—TOO serviee was also taken part in by the Rey. John Yong, Mr. T. Wal- lington, and Per. Healey. A collection was taken on be- half of the society's funds.

His cemplei at against church- men was that they were too low, they stood net near enough to the New Testament ideal of churchminship. PRESENTATION AT NEWTON.

On Saturday evening there was a good number of the members and friends of the Makerfield Harriers at the White Lion Hotel, Newton, on the occasion of a presentation to Mr. James Williamson, late secre- tary of the Harriers' club, and formerly police Con- stable at Newton, who has been transferred to Dean near Bolton. A substantial knife and fork tea was first partaken of and at the subsequent proceedings, Councillor John Simpson in making the presents-' tion referred t 3 Mr. Williamson's indefatigable exertions on behalf of the club, and added that their recognition of them that evening had been limited to the members of the club. He had great pleasure in asking their late Secretary's acceptance of a very handsome black marble timepiece, which be hoped would serve as a memento of the time he had spent amongst them. In reply. Mr, Wdliam• son said he had hoaestly done his best since the for- mation of the club to further its interests. He urged the members to continue to make the club a successful athletic organisation in the district. He was pleased to meet so many of his ol 1 friends in the club, as well as those outside the ranks of the harriers present. In conclusion he assured them that he would treasure the gift and would always coaple it in his thoughts with the time he had spent in connection with the harriers and in the Newton district. Various toasts, including the "President," " The Makerfield Harriers," " The Visitors," " The Press," " Host and Hostess," were proposed and duly honoured.

He remarked at the outset that the application was not provided for in the section under which it was made. THE VISIT OF Mlt. FIENRY BROID- HURST, ALP., TO WA ttitINTOTON.

As most of our readers are by this time aware, Mr. Henry Broadhurst, will address a meeting of Liberais in the Parr Hall, Warrington, on Wed- nesday evening next. Some considerable time has elapsed since Mr. Broadhurst last addressed a meet- ing in Warrington, but there are many peop,e who still remember with pleasure his last visit. At the meeting on Wednesday evening, the leading Llberals in the town and district wih he present. Councillor John Crosfi-13, J.P., will preside, and will take the chair at 7,45 p.m. The doors will be open at 7 o'clock. Mr. Breadherst is a thoroueh workingman's representative in Parliament, he having himself risen from the ranks. He is the son of a journeyman sonemason. and was born at , Littlemore, near Oxford, in 1840. Me received some little education at a village school there. He worked as a joerneyman stonemason up to the year 1872, when he became.secretary of the Labour Reprelentation Lsaus. In 1875 he was appointed Secretary of the' Parliamentary Committee of the Trades Dolan Congress. During the agitation on the Eastern question he took a leading part in the organization of meetings, &c, in support of Mr. 01 idstone's policy. He was elected member of Parliament for 'Stoke-on-Trent in 1880, and did some very useful work as a member of the Royal Commission, on the housing of the working-classes in 1881-5. At the general election of 1885 he we returned for the B ordestay division of Birmingham. In February, 1886, he was appointed Under- Secretary of State for the Home Department in Mr. Oladstone'e ministry. At the general election of 1886, h 3 successfully stood for West Nottingham, but was not re-elected in 1892. He took a leading pln in the passing of the Employers' Liability Act, 1830, and many other measures affecting the industrial classes. He is the author of the Laaeehald Enfranchisement Bill, and during the sessions of 1881-5 he had charge of the Deceased's Wife's Sister Bill. He has always been a prominent advocate or 011 Age Pansions,and will no doubt have somethine to say on that subject on Wednesday evening. After his defeat at West Not- tingham in 1892, Mr. Broadhurst did nat re-enter Parliament until 1891 when he was electel a mem- ber for Leicester on the double vacancy caused by the retirement of Sir T. Whitehead and Mr. T. A. Pickton. He has continued to be one of the mem- bers tor Leicester since that time.

Winter's Approach. since the annual scourge of Influenza first wrought havoc amongst us, the dread of winter's approach, with some persons, has reached an acute stage. Especially is this so with those who are liable to colds and chills, those who are habitually exposed to the inclemencies of the weather, and those whose general health is not robust. With many persons an acquaintance with Bovril has served to allay this dread of coming winter. The warmth-giving, strength-giving, stimulative properties of this highly-condensed food—which has been aptly described as "liquid. fire"—are of the greatest value to all, to the strong as well as to the weak—to the adult and to the child. jwc • : „7;,,- 4,4", ; • - - • ' 4.; c,, = ; • : ebN provides that natural warmth which only a true and really stimulative food can give, and so serves to ward off colds and chills, the ever- lurking danger of Influenza, and the attacks of disease ess easy to repulse when the body is not in good condition. In the months of fogs, mists, and chilly cold, Bovril is a veritable safe- guard, which none should be without. Bovril is prepared from the primest parts of the most richly pastured cattle in the world. It is the most perfect fc :n strengthening, nourishing, and easily digested food in the smallest possible bulk. Bovril is, in fact, the vital principle of prime Ox Beef. Bovril is Liquid Life.

THE REV. F. AND MRS, SLATER'S DEPARTURE. Toe Rev. Francis Slater, M.A., who has left War- rington this week to take charge of the parish of St. Chad's, Over, preached his farewell sermon in St. James' Church, Latchford. on Sunday evening, to a very large congregation. On Monday evening the rev. gentleman and Mrs. Slater, had several handsome presentations made to them,Mrs. Slater receiving a gold bracelet,she and her husband a purl coat lining £B2, and the Vicar a beautifully illuminated address. The presentations were made on behalf of numeroui parishioners and personal friends by the Vicar's warden (sir. C. S. Burgess). The Vicar, who spoke under deep emotion, suitably replied, and a very interesting musical programme was gone through.

THE DREYFUS CASE. TWENTY PERSONS INJURED AT A MEETING. MARSEILLES, THURSDAY. M. de Pressense gave a lecture here yesterday on the Dreyfus affair. The meeting proved to be of a very dis iderly character. A series of affrays took plane, in the course ot which twenty persons were injured, none of them, however, seriously. Several arrests were made. The meeting ultimately adopted a resolution de- mending the abandonment of the proceedings against , Colonel Piequart and the abolition of the court mar- tial. 1 A DESTRUCTIVE CYCLONE. ROME, THURSDAY. A destructive cyclone has caused great damage in the neighbourhood of Pauli Pirri, in Sardinia, About 3o- houses were blown down. Several people ware killed and injured.
